### PilotRL: Training Language Model Agents via Global Planning-Guided Progressive Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.00344v1)
- **Authors**: Keer Lu, Chong Chen, Bin Cui, Huang Leng, Wentao Zhang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable advancements in tackling agent-oriented tasks. Despite their potential, existing work faces challenges when deploying LLMs in agent-based environments. The widely adopted agent paradigm ReAct centers on integrating single-step reasoning with immediate action execution, which limits its effectiveness in complex tasks requiring long-term strategic planning. Furthermore, the coordination between the planner and executor during problem-solving is also a critical factor to consider in agent design. Additionally, current approaches predominantly rely on supervised fine-tuning, which often leads models to memorize established task completion trajectories, thereby restricting their generalization ability when confronted with novel problem contexts. To address these challenges, we introduce an adaptive global plan-based agent paradigm AdaPlan, aiming to synergize high-level explicit guidance with execution to support effective long-horizon decision-making. Based on the proposed paradigm, we further put forward PilotRL, a global planning-guided training framework for LLM agents driven by progressive reinforcement learning. We first develop the model's ability to follow explicit guidance from global plans when addressing agent tasks. Subsequently, based on this foundation, we focus on optimizing the quality of generated plans. Finally, we conduct joint optimization of the model's planning and execution coordination. Experiments indicate that PilotRL could achieve state-of-the-art performances, with LLaMA3.1-8B-Instruct + PilotRL surpassing closed-sourced GPT-4o by 3.60%, while showing a more substantial gain of 55.78% comparing to GPT-4o-mini at a comparable parameter scale.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PilotRL: Training Language Model Agents via Global Planning-Guided Progressive Reinforcement Learning":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PilotRL, a novel training framework that aims to improve the capabilities of Large Language Model (LLM) agents in complex tasks. PilotRL tackles the limitations of existing agent paradigms like ReAct, which struggles with long-term strategic planning and coordination between planning and execution. The core idea is to leverage an adaptive global plan-based paradigm called AdaPlan. AdaPlan synergizes high-level explicit guidance with execution, enabling more effective long-horizon decision-making. PilotRL employs a progressive reinforcement learning approach divided into three stages: 1) Executor Enhancement, focusing on the executor's adherence to the global plan; 2) Global Planner Cultivation, optimizing the quality of generated plans; and 3) Joint Optimization, refining the coordination between planning and execution. The paper presents experimental results demonstrating PilotRL's superiority over existing methods, even surpassing some closed-source models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of ideas that, while not entirely individually novel, constitute a meaningful contribution when combined. The progressive RL training schedule, coupled with the adaptive global planning mechanism (AdaPlan), is a unique approach. Existing methods either focus on single-step reasoning (ReAct), or rely on supervised fine-tuning, which the paper argues limits generalization. The approach of breaking down the RL process into stages is a solid improvement over existing methods.
    *   **Strength:** The novelty lies in the *combination* of elements: the explicit global plan guidance, adaptive refinement of plans based on feedback, and the progressive reinforcement learning strategy for training the agent.
    *   **Weakness:** Each component by itself builds upon prior research, such as RLHF or methods for tool use. The AdaPlan paradigm also has some overlap with existing works on long-horizon reasoning.

*   **Significance:** The problem the paper addresses -- improving LLM agents' ability to perform complex tasks requiring strategic planning -- is highly relevant. The gains achieved relative to established baselines, and even surpassing certain closed-source models, are significant and indicate the potential of the proposed approach.
    *   **Strength:** LLM agents are a growing area of research, and the ability to scale opensource LLMs to outperform proprietary models is a significant advancement. If the gains hold with other tasks and setups, it's a great achievement.
    *   **Weakness:** The significance depends on whether these specific gains are replicable across diverse tasks and domains. The generalizability of the learned policy, even with RL, should be carefully examined. Also, the improvement over GPT-4o is mentioned but not heavily focused on, perhaps because it is less significant than the improvement over GPT-4o-mini.

*   **Methodological Rigor:** The paper outlines the approach clearly and provides sufficient experimental details. However, some aspects could be strengthened.
    *   **Strength:** The three-stage training methodology is well-defined and justified.
    *   **Weakness:** A more detailed analysis of the types of errors made by the different agents would be beneficial. Also, some parts are a bit hand-wavy, like the model evaluation. Specifically the design and implementation of the reward design is not rigorous enough, and it relies heavily on LLM-as-Judge, which could be biased.

*   **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is generally well-written and organized. The introduction clearly motivates the work, and the proposed approach is explained logically.
    *   **Strength:**  The figures are informative and help to illustrate the key concepts.
    *   **Weakness:** The Appendix is long and could be shortened and integrated better.

*   **Potential Influence:** The PilotRL framework has the potential to influence future research on LLM agents. The focus on explicit planning and progressive reinforcement learning provides a valuable direction for improving agent capabilities. The progressive training paradigm of PilotRL could be generalized to other agent-based tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**.

*   It presents a novel and useful combination of existing ideas, resulting in a clear improvement over established baselines.
*   The problem being addressed is highly relevant, and the potential impact on the field of LLM agents is notable.
*   The methodology has some weaknesses in the reward function, so more rigorous study is necessary to be completely certain of the results. The robustness and generalization of the framework warrant further exploration.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### BOOD: Boundary-based Out-Of-Distribution Data Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.00350v1)
- **Authors**: Qilin Liao, Shuo Yang, Bo Zhao, Ping Luo, Hengshuang Zhao
- **Abstract**: Harnessing the power of diffusion models to synthesize auxiliary training data based on latent space features has proven effective in enhancing out-of-distribution (OOD) detection performance. However, extracting effective features outside the in-distribution (ID) boundary in latent space remains challenging due to the difficulty of identifying decision boundaries between classes. This paper proposes a novel framework called Boundary-based Out-Of-Distribution data generation (BOOD), which synthesizes high-quality OOD features and generates human-compatible outlier images using diffusion models. BOOD first learns a text-conditioned latent feature space from the ID dataset, selects ID features closest to the decision boundary, and perturbs them to cross the decision boundary to form OOD features. These synthetic OOD features are then decoded into images in pixel space by a diffusion model. Compared to previous works, BOOD provides a more training efficient strategy for synthesizing informative OOD features, facilitating clearer distinctions between ID and OOD data. Extensive experimental results on common benchmarks demonstrate that BOOD surpasses the state-of-the-art method significantly, achieving a 29.64% decrease in average FPR95 (40.31% vs. 10.67%) and a 7.27% improvement in average AUROC (90.15% vs. 97.42%) on the CIFAR-100 dataset.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "BOOD: Boundary-based Out-Of-Distribution Data Generation" addresses the problem of generating effective out-of-distribution (OOD) data for improving OOD detection in machine learning models.  The core idea is to synthesize OOD data points that lie close to the decision boundaries between classes in the latent space. This is achieved through a novel framework called Boundary-based Out-Of-Distribution data generation (BOOD), which:

1.  Learns a text-conditioned latent feature space from the in-distribution (ID) dataset.
2.  Identifies ID features that are closest to the decision boundary using an adversarial perturbation strategy.  The minimal number of perturbation steps to change the model's prediction is used as a proxy for distance to the boundary.
3.  Perturbs these boundary ID features along the gradient ascent direction to cross the decision boundary, creating synthetic OOD features.
4.  Decodes these synthetic OOD features into pixel-space OOD images using a diffusion model.
The paper demonstrates significant improvements in OOD detection performance on CIFAR-100 and IMAGENET-100 datasets compared to existing methods, including a substantial reduction in FPR95 and increase in AUROC.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the explicit focus on generating OOD data *near* the decision boundaries.  Existing approaches like DreamOOD generate OOD data from low-likelihood regions, which might not be the most informative for refining the ID/OOD boundary. The use of adversarial perturbation to approximate the distance to the decision boundary and guide OOD data generation is a significant contribution. The class embedding alignment with diffusion models to provide high quality images is also beneficial.

*   **Significance:** Improving OOD detection is crucial for deploying machine learning models in open-world settings.  By generating more informative OOD data, BOOD enables models to learn better decision boundaries and reduce the risk of incorrect predictions on unseen data. The performance gains reported in the paper are substantial, indicating the practical value of the proposed approach.  This has the potential to make downstream tasks such as anomaly detection safer.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing OOD data generation methods.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** BOOD is presented as a coherent and well-justified framework.  The individual components (latent space learning, boundary identification, OOD feature synthesis, image generation) are clearly explained.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experimental results on CIFAR-100 and ImageNet-100 demonstrate the effectiveness of BOOD, surpassing state-of-the-art methods by a significant margin.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of different components of BOOD (boundary identification, feature perturbation).
    *   **Computational Efficiency Analysis:** The authors addressed the computational cost of their method, comparing it with others.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The framework introduces additional hyperparameters (e.g., step size, perturbation steps, pruning rate). While the paper explores the impact of these parameters, the process of tuning them in practice might be challenging and could limit the generalizability of the approach. BOOD's performance might be heavily reliant on carefully chosen hyperparameter settings.
    *   **Reliance on Diffusion Models:** The quality of the generated OOD images depends on the capabilities of the underlying diffusion model.  If the diffusion model struggles to generate realistic images for certain classes or domains, the effectiveness of BOOD might be reduced. There might be domain-specific limitations.
    *   **Implicit Assumption on Boundary Quality:** The paper assumes that the decision boundaries learned from the ID data are reasonably accurate. If the ID data is noisy or biased, the identified boundaries might be suboptimal, leading to the generation of less effective OOD data. This may not always hold and the assumptions on boundary quality could be investigated further.

*   **Potential Influence:** BOOD's explicit focus on decision boundaries has the potential to inspire further research in OOD data generation. Future work could explore more sophisticated methods for identifying and characterizing decision boundaries, as well as adaptive strategies for tuning the hyperparameters of the OOD generation process. In addition, the framework of BOOD could be applied to various fields that employ OOD detection such as medical imaging.

**Score Rationale:**

I assign a score of **8**. While the paper builds upon existing techniques (diffusion models, adversarial perturbations), it makes a significant contribution by focusing on the explicit generation of OOD data near decision boundaries. The experimental results are compelling, and the ablation studies provide valuable insights. The hyperparameter sensitivity and reliance on diffusion models are limitations, but the overall impact of the paper is substantial. BOOD represents a significant advance in OOD data generation and has the potential to influence future research in this area.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Representation Shift: Unifying Token Compression with FlashAttention
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.00367v1)
- **Authors**: Joonmyung Choi, Sanghyeok Lee, Byungoh Ko, Eunseo Kim, Jihyung Kil, Hyunwoo J. Kim
- **Abstract**: Transformers have demonstrated remarkable success across vision, language, and video. Yet, increasing task complexity has led to larger models and more tokens, raising the quadratic cost of self-attention and the overhead of GPU memory access. To reduce the computation cost of self-attention, prior work has proposed token compression techniques that drop redundant or less informative tokens. Meanwhile, fused attention kernels such as FlashAttention have been developed to alleviate memory overhead by avoiding attention map construction and its associated I/O to HBM. This, however, makes it incompatible with most training-free token compression methods, which rely on attention maps to determine token importance. Here, we propose Representation Shift, a training-free, model-agnostic metric that measures the degree of change in each token's representation. This seamlessly integrates token compression with FlashAttention, without attention maps or retraining. Our method further generalizes beyond Transformers to CNNs and state space models. Extensive experiments show that Representation Shift enables effective token compression compatible with FlashAttention, yielding significant speedups of up to 5.5% and 4.4% in video-text retrieval and video QA, respectively. Code is available at https://github.com/mlvlab/Representation-Shift.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Representation Shift," a novel, training-free, and model-agnostic metric for token importance in neural networks, particularly Vision Transformers (ViTs). The core idea is to quantify the change in a token's representation as it passes through a network layer, hypothesizing that significant shifts indicate more important tokens. This metric is used for token compression (pruning less important tokens) to accelerate inference.  A key contribution is the compatibility with FlashAttention, a memory-optimized attention mechanism that is traditionally incompatible with attention-map-dependent token pruning methods.  The authors demonstrate significant speedups on video and image understanding tasks, including video-text retrieval and video question answering, while maintaining or even improving accuracy.  They also show the generalizability of Representation Shift to CNNs and State Space Models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of using the "representation shift" as a proxy for token importance is moderately novel.  While previous works have explored attention-based methods and learnable networks for token pruning, this approach is a fresh perspective that avoids the limitations of relying on attention maps or requiring retraining.  The model-agnostic nature and seamless integration with FlashAttention are also significant novelties.  While the L2 distance metric itself isn't new, its application to quantify token importance in this context is a worthwhile contribution.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing a practical problem: accelerating ViTs without sacrificing accuracy or requiring extensive retraining.  FlashAttention provides substantial speedups, but its incompatibility with existing token pruning methods limits its full potential.  Representation Shift effectively bridges this gap, providing a general and simple way to combine both techniques for greater efficiency.  The application to CNNs and SSMs extends the impact beyond transformers and indicates a broader usefulness for the technique. The observed speedups (up to 5.5x) are substantial and could have a meaningful impact on the deployment of ViTs and other models in resource-constrained environments. The potential to be implemented in different architectures makes this method very versatile.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Simplicity and Effectiveness:** The Representation Shift metric is easy to compute and understand, yet it yields impressive results.
    * **Compatibility with FlashAttention:** This is a key selling point, as it allows for the combination of two important acceleration techniques.
    * **Model-Agnostic:** Extends beyond Transformers to CNNs and SSMs.
    * **Training-Free:** Avoids the overhead of training additional networks for token importance.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** Extensive experiments demonstrate significant speedups and accuracy improvements on various tasks.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Theoretical Justification:** While the experimental results are compelling, the paper lacks a deeper theoretical justification for why Representation Shift effectively captures token importance. A more thorough analysis of the relationship between representation change and information content would strengthen the paper.
    * **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of the sensitivity of the results to the choice of distance metric (L2 norm) and the layers used for computing the representation shift.
    * **Limited Comparison with SOTA Pruning Techniques:** A more comprehensive comparison with other existing token pruning methods, particularly in terms of computational overhead and implementation complexity, would provide a better understanding of the advantages and disadvantages of Representation Shift.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on efficient ViTs and other neural network architectures. The approach is generalizable and easy to implement, which should encourage wider adoption and exploration. It opens up new avenues for combining token pruning with memory-optimized attention mechanisms.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel and effective approach to token compression, some limitations prevent it from being considered a groundbreaking contribution. The theoretical justification could be strengthened, and the experimental comparison with existing methods could be more comprehensive. However, the practical benefits (speedups, compatibility with FlashAttention, model-agnostic) are significant. I think this paper is a very good contribution, and is likely to be used in future work to help compress tokens.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### EdgeInfinite-Instruct: Bridging SFT-Based Optimization and NPU-Level Efficiency for Edge Devices
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.00370v1)
- **Authors**: Jiyu Chen, Poh Seng Lim, Shuang Peng, Daxiong Luo, JungHau Foo, Yap Deep, Timothy Lee Jun Jie, Kelvin Teh Kae Wen, Fan Yang, Danyu Feng, Hao-Yun Chen, Peng-Wen Chen, Fangyuan Li, Xiaoxin Chen, Wong Wai Mun
- **Abstract**: Deploying Transformer-based large language models (LLMs) on resource-constrained edge devices for long-sequence tasks remains challenging due to the quadratic time complexity of self-attention and growing Key-Value (KV) cache demands. While existing KV cache optimizations improve memory efficiency, they often fail to reduce time to first token (TTFT) and may degrade performance through token pruning. Alternative sequence modeling architectures address some of these limitations, but typically require full retraining and lack infrastructure support. EdgeInfinite offers an efficient solution by fine-tuning only a small subset of parameters, maintaining quality while reducing both computational and memory costs, including improved TTFT. However, its instruction-following ability is limited, and it lacks mobile-specific optimizations. To address these issues, we propose EdgeInfinite-Instruct, which introduces a Segmented Supervised Fine-Tuning (S-SFT) strategy tailored to long-sequence tasks such as summarization and question answering. We further optimized EdgeInfinite-Instruct for efficient deployment on edge NPUs by employing fine-grained post-training quantization (PTQ) to reduce computational demands while maintaining accuracy, and by implementing a fixed-shape computation graph that balances memory usage and on-device efficiency through scenario-specific customization of input token and cache sizes. Experiments on long-context benchmarks and real-world mobile tasks show that our approach improves domain-specific performance while maintaining efficiency on NPU-accelerated edge devices.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EdgeInfinite-Instruct, an improved version of EdgeInfinite designed for deploying large language models (LLMs) on resource-constrained edge devices. It addresses limitations of the original EdgeInfinite, specifically improving instruction-following capabilities and optimizing for mobile Neural Processing Units (NPUs). Key contributions include:

*   **Segmented Supervised Fine-Tuning (S-SFT):** A novel fine-tuning strategy tailored to EdgeInfinite's architecture, enhancing instruction-following abilities and overall performance.
*   **NPU Optimization:** Techniques such as post-training quantization (PTQ) and fixed-shape graph implementation to improve computational efficiency on NPUs.
*   **Empirical Evaluation:** Demonstrations of improved performance on long-context benchmarks and real-world mobile tasks, showing reduced latency and memory usage while maintaining accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a significant problem: deploying LLMs effectively on edge devices. The quadratic complexity of Transformer attention and the increasing memory requirements of KV caches pose major challenges. The paper builds upon the existing EdgeInfinite framework to provide a more practical and performant solution.

**Strengths:**

*   **Practical Relevance:** The paper focuses on real-world deployment, addressing the limitations of existing LLM optimization techniques for edge devices.
*   **Novel S-SFT Approach:** The proposed fine-tuning strategy is tailored to the EdgeInfinite architecture and demonstrates improved instruction-following capabilities, a key area for practical LLM applications.
*   **NPU Optimization:** The paper provides concrete techniques for optimizing LLMs for NPU deployment, including quantization and fixed-shape graph implementation. This is a significant contribution as it provides specific guidance for practitioners.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes experiments on standard benchmarks (LongBench) and real-world mobile tasks, demonstrating the effectiveness of the proposed approach in diverse scenarios.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Optimization Techniques:** While the application of PTQ and fixed-shape graphs is well-motivated, these techniques are not entirely novel in themselves. The specific implementation details and their integration with S-SFT and EdgeInfinite are what add value.
*   **Single-Document QA Performance:** The paper acknowledges that EdgeInfinite-Instruct underperforms on single-document QA tasks due to information loss during memory compression. This limitation should be emphasized and further investigated. It might limit the applicability of this research for the kind of application where the full context is imperative.
*   **Incremental Improvement:** EdgeInfinite-Instruct is an improvement over EdgeInfinite, but the underlying approach is similar. The paper should clearly articulate the differences, and novelty relative to EdgeInfinite, not the overall state of the art.

**Significance:**

The paper is significant because it provides a practical and efficient approach for deploying LLMs on edge devices. The S-SFT training method and NPU optimization techniques enable the model to achieve good performance on long-context tasks while minimizing memory usage and latency. It’s particularly important because it improves the privacy in data sensitive application.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field of efficient LLM deployment. It's not a groundbreaking theoretical advance, but it provides valuable practical techniques and empirical evidence for deploying LLMs on resource-constrained devices. It also addresses a critical practical challenge. There's some novelty around segmented-SFT training. However, the quantization methods are existing ones.

### Bidirectional Action Sequence Learning for Long-term Action Anticipation with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.00374v1)
- **Authors**: Yuji Sato, Yasunori Ishii, Takayoshi Yamashita
- **Abstract**: Video-based long-term action anticipation is crucial for early risk detection in areas such as automated driving and robotics. Conventional approaches extract features from past actions using encoders and predict future events with decoders, which limits performance due to their unidirectional nature. These methods struggle to capture semantically distinct sub-actions within a scene. The proposed method, BiAnt, addresses this limitation by combining forward prediction with backward prediction using a large language model. Experimental results on Ego4D demonstrate that BiAnt improves performance in terms of edit distance compared to baseline methods.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper based on the information provided in the extracted text.

**Summary:**

The paper "Bidirectional Action Sequence Learning for Long-term Action Anticipation with Large Language Models" introduces BiAnt, a new framework for improving long-term action anticipation in videos. The key idea is to leverage a large language model (LLM) trained with both forward (past to future) and backward (future to past) prediction tasks.  The authors argue that traditional methods using only forward prediction struggle to capture the context and continuity needed for understanding complex actions, especially those with distinct sub-actions. BiAnt aims to address this by enabling the LLM to learn bidirectional dependencies between past and future actions, creating a more robust understanding of the scene context. Experiments on the Ego4D dataset demonstrate that BiAnt improves performance in terms of edit distance compared to baseline methods that use only forward prediction. The paper also explores ablations on the task description and interval lengths.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core novelty of the paper lies in the **integration of bidirectional learning with a large language model for long-term action anticipation**. While LLMs have been used for action anticipation before (e.g., AntGPT), the addition of a backward prediction task to enhance context understanding is the primary contribution.  Bidirectional learning itself is not new (especially in NLP), but its application in this specific domain, leveraging LLMs, appears to be a novel approach. Furthermore, the paper introduces a simple yet effective method of using special tokens in the prompt for each of the forward and backward tasks.
*   **Significance:** Long-term action anticipation is a crucial area with practical applications in automated driving, robotics, and anomaly detection. Improving the accuracy and robustness of these systems can have significant real-world impact. The paper's results on the Ego4D dataset suggest that BiAnt can indeed improve performance compared to existing methods. The improvement is especially clear in complex scenes where the actions do not follow an intuitive causal sequence.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing unidirectional approaches for long-term action anticipation.
    *   **Well-Defined Method:** The BiAnt framework is well-explained, with clear details on how the forward and backward prediction tasks are integrated.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Experiments on Ego4D provide evidence that BiAnt can improve performance. The qualitative analysis highlights scenarios where BiAnt excels in capturing the flow of actions, which is not attainable by current forward-only prediction methods.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** Ablation studies explore the impact of the important factors, such as the special tokens in prompts, and observation intervals, therefore improving the reliability and impact of the findings.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the results show improvement, the gains in edit distance might not be substantial enough to be considered a game-changer. The exact magnitude of the improvement and its statistical significance needs to be carefully examined in the actual paper.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The paper mainly focuses on Ego4D dataset. Generalizability is an important consideration. The paper would be more impactful if it were tested with different datasets to show that it is generally applicable to the action anticipation task.
    *   **LLM dependence:** The performance gains may be highly contingent on the specific LLM used (Llama2-7B in this case). Performance with other LLMs would strengthen the generalizability of the approach.
    *   **Lack of explanation for parameters:** The loss parameters are set to a default of 1.0, which gives an impression that the parameters have not been finely tuned.

* **Impact:**
    * The paper introduces a new framework, which has implications for future studies on action anticipation and scene context.
    * The framework is simple and effective in nature, making it easy to integrate into current action anticipation architectures.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7** to this paper.

Here's the rationale:

*   **The idea of using bidirectional learning with LLMs for action anticipation has merit and shows promising initial results, providing a novel direction for this approach.**
*   The paper is clear in its problem definition, methodology, and experimental setup.
*   The weakness is that the results can be attributed to LLM parameters, and the gains on the primary benchmark dataset (Ego4D) might not be overwhelmingly large and can be further improved through better tuning of parameters. Furthermore, more datasets should be tested to show how generally applicable the framework is.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### CoRGI: Verified Chain-of-Thought Reasoning with Visual Grounding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.00378v1)
- **Authors**: Shixin Yi, Lin Shang
- **Abstract**: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting has shown promise in improving reasoning in vision-language models (VLMs), but it often produces explanations that are linguistically fluent yet lack grounding in visual content. We observe that such hallucinations arise in part from the absence of an explicit verification mechanism during multi-step reasoning. To address this, we propose \textbf{CoRGI}(\textbf{C}hain \textbf{o}f \textbf{R}easoning with \textbf{G}rounded \textbf{I}nsights), a modular framework that introduces visual verification into the reasoning process. CoRGI follows a three-stage pipeline: it first generates a textual reasoning chain, then extracts supporting visual evidence for each reasoning step via a dedicated module (VEVM), and finally synthesizes the textual rationale with visual evidence to generate a grounded, verified answer. The framework can be integrated with existing VLMs without end-to-end retraining. We evaluate CoRGI on the VCR benchmark and find that it improves reasoning performance on two representative open-source VLM backbones, Qwen-2.5VL and LLaVA-1.6. Ablation studies confirm the contribution of each step in the verification module, and human evaluations suggest that CoRGI leads to more factual and helpful explanations. We also examine alternative designs for the visual verification step and discuss potential limitations of post-hoc verification frameworks. These findings highlight the importance of grounding intermediate reasoning steps in visual evidence to enhance the robustness of multimodal reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "CoRGI: Verified Chain-of-Thought Reasoning with Visual Grounding" addresses the problem of hallucinations in vision-language models (VLMs) when using Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting. The authors argue that these hallucinations arise from a lack of explicit visual verification during the reasoning process. To address this, they propose CoRGI (Chain of Reasoning with Grounded Insights), a modular framework that injects visual verification into the reasoning pipeline. CoRGI consists of three stages: (1) generating a textual reasoning chain, (2) extracting supporting visual evidence for each reasoning step using a dedicated Visual Evidence Verification Module (VEVM), and (3) synthesizing the textual rationale with the visual evidence to generate a grounded answer.  The VEVM module includes a relevance classifier, an RoI selection mechanism, and a VLM-based visual fact-checker. The framework is designed to be integrated with existing VLMs without end-to-end retraining.  Experiments on the VCR benchmark demonstrate improvements in reasoning performance compared to baseline VLMs and CoT prompting. Human evaluations suggest that CORGI produces more factual and helpful explanations. The paper also includes ablation studies and explores alternative designs for the visual verification step.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents a reasonably novel approach by explicitly incorporating a visual verification step into the CoT reasoning process for VLMs. While the idea of grounding language in visual evidence is not entirely new, the structured three-stage pipeline, particularly the modular VEVM with its relevance classifier, RoI selection, and visual fact-checker, provides a specific and practical implementation. The framework's ability to be integrated with existing VLMs *without* end-to-end retraining is also a valuable feature.  However, the novelty is somewhat diminished by the fact that it relies on existing components (VLMs, object detectors). The combination and integration are where the primary novelty lies.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to improve the reliability and trustworthiness of VLMs, especially in applications where explainability is crucial. By mitigating hallucinations, the framework addresses a critical limitation of existing CoT-augmented VLMs. The performance gains on the VCR benchmark, while not massive, are consistent across different VLM backbones, indicating robustness. The human evaluation results further bolster the claim that CoRGI produces more useful and trustworthy explanations. The modular design promotes adoption and experimentation by the research community.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the issue of hallucinations in VLMs.
    *   **Well-defined framework:**  The CoRGI framework is well-structured and modular, making it easy to understand and implement.
    *   **Practical design:** The emphasis on using existing components and avoiding end-to-end retraining is a significant advantage.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:**  The paper includes quantitative experiments, ablation studies, and human evaluations, providing a thorough assessment of the framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on existing components:** The performance is inherently limited by the performance of the underlying VLMs and object detectors.
    *   **Post-hoc verification:**  The verification step is applied *after* the reasoning chain is generated, which limits the ability to correct errors in real-time.  This is a significant limitation discussed in the conclusion.
    *   **Limited generalization assessment:**  The main experiments focus on the VCR dataset.  While there is a mention of VQA-v2 (only qualitative example),  a broader assessment of generalization capability would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Minor Limitations:**  The evaluation of CoT prompting and the performance drop it causes for Qwen is not explored to great detail. The statistical significance of changes from different ablations is not included.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in multimodal reasoning by highlighting the importance of visual grounding and providing a practical framework for incorporating verification into the reasoning process. It could inspire further work on tighter integration between reasoning and perception, as well as the development of more specialized verification modules.  The code release will likely encourage adoption and further development by the community.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's solid contribution to the field. The paper presents a novel and well-defined framework that addresses a practical problem in VLMs. The performance gains, while moderate, are consistent and supported by human evaluations. The modular design and practical approach increase its potential for adoption and impact. However, the reliance on existing components, the post-hoc nature of the verification, and the limited generalization assessment prevent it from achieving a higher score. While it effectively uses existing VLM components to improve the accuracy of the results, it has limited novelty from a fundamental algorithm or methodology point of view.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### STF: Shallow-Level Temporal Feedback to Enhance Spiking Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.00387v1)
- **Authors**: Zeqi Zheng, Zizheng Zhu, Yingchao Yu, Yanchen Huang, Changze Lv, Junfeng Tang, Zhaofei Yu, Yaochu Jin
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) suffer from a great performance gap compared to floating-point Artificial Neural Networks (ANNs) due to the binary nature of spike trains. Recent efforts have introduced deep-level feedback loops to transmit high-level semantic information to narrow this gap. However, these designs often span multiple deep layers, resulting in costly feature transformations, higher parameter overhead, increased energy consumption, and longer inference latency. To address this issue, we propose Shallow-level Temporal Feedback (STF), a lightweight plug-and-play module for the encoding layer, which consists of Temporal-Spatial Position Embedding (TSPE) and Temporal Feedback (TF).Extensive experiments show that STF consistently improves performance across various Transformer-based SNN backbones on static datasets, including CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet-1K, under different spike timestep settings. Further analysis reveals that STF enhances the diversity of the spike patterns, which is key to performance gain. Moreover, evaluations on adversarial robustness and temporal sensitivity confirm that STF outperforms direct coding and its variants, highlighting its potential as a new spike encoding scheme for static scenarios. Our code will be released upon acceptance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a Shallow-level Temporal Feedback (STF) module to improve the performance of Transformer-based Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs). The STF module, consisting of Temporal-Spatial Position Embedding (TSPE) and Temporal Feedback (TF), is designed to be a lightweight, plug-and-play component integrated into the encoding layer of SNNs. The paper demonstrates through experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and ImageNet-1K datasets that STF enhances performance across various SNN backbones and spike timestep settings. The authors argue that STF improves performance by enhancing the diversity of spike patterns and providing richer spike-based representations, which mitigates the significant performance gap compared to ANNs. The paper also investigates the robustness and temporal encoding characteristics of the STF scheme, comparing it with direct coding and its variants.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of incorporating feedback loops into SNNs isn't entirely new. Existing works like SpiLiFormer and TDFormer have explored deep-level feedback mechanisms. The *novelty* of this paper lies in its focus on *shallow-level* feedback. By placing the feedback mechanism in the encoding layer, the authors aim to mitigate computational overhead and latency associated with deeper feedback loops. However, it is an incremental advancement over the existing methods, not a completely new paradigm.
*   **Significance:** The significance of this paper is mixed. The empirical results demonstrating consistent performance improvements across multiple datasets and SNN backbones are encouraging. The analysis of spike pattern diversity as a key factor contributing to STF's performance is insightful. Further, the demonstration that STF can help QKFormer approach the performance of much larger models such as E-Spikformer is a significant finding. The robustness evaluations and assessment of temporal information preservation add value. However, The performance degradation on neuromorphic datasets and its limited applicability to static scenarios is a major drawback.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined and motivated problem.
    *   Clear and concise explanation of the proposed method.
    *   Thorough experimental validation across multiple datasets and architectures, with ablation studies on the modules.
    *   Insightful analysis of spike pattern diversity and its role in performance gain.
    *   Demonstration of robustness gains under adversarial attacks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The improvement in performance is marginal in some cases.
    *   The increase in energy consumption and inference latency, while relatively small, is still a concern.
    *   Limited applicability to static data. STF degrades the performance on neuromorphic datasets, highlighting a potential conflict with inherent temporal structures in event-based data.
    *   Inference latency has increased by approximately 10%, which diminishes its value in real-world applications.
    *   While the diversity analysis is insightful, deeper mechanistic explanations for *why* shallow-level feedback specifically improves spike pattern diversity are lacking.
    *   STF appears to lack scalability to even more complex and computationally expensive tasks.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper presents an interesting and well-executed investigation into shallow-level temporal feedback for SNNs. The empirical results are compelling, and the analysis of spike pattern diversity offers valuable insights. However, the incremental nature of the novelty, the marginal performance improvements in some cases, and limited applicability to static scenarios prevent a higher score. The paper represents a solid contribution to the field of SNNs, but its limitations preclude it from being a truly groundbreaking or transformative work.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.NE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Benchmarking LLMs for Unit Test Generation from Real-World Functions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.00408v1)
- **Authors**: Dong Huang, Jie M. Zhang, Mark Harman, Qianru Zhang, Mingzhe Du, See-Kiong Ng
- **Abstract**: Recently, large language models (LLMs) have shown great promise in automating unit test generation, significantly reducing the manual effort required by developers. To effectively evaluate the capabilities of LLMs in this domain, it is crucial to have a well-designed benchmark that accurately reflects real-world scenarios and mitigates common pitfalls. Existing LLM test generation benchmarks are limited by two critical drawbacks: data contamination and structurally simple function code. As a result, we often cannot rely on the validity of scientific conclusions drawn from empirical studies using these limited benchmarks. The empirical evidence presented may be biased due to contamination and may fail to generalize beyond toy programs due to structural simplicity. To address these problems, we introduce ULT (UnLeakedTestbench), a new benchmark specifically designed for function-level unit test generation from real-world Python functions. ULT is constructed through a multi-stage curation process that ensures high cyclomatic complexity and mitigates test case contamination. With 3,909 carefully selected function-level tasks, ULT provides a more realistic and challenging evaluation of LLMs' test generation capabilities. We also provide PLT (PreLeakedTestbench), a pair benchmark of ULT with leaked tests designed to enable a controlled analysis of memorization versus reasoning in test generation. Our evaluation results demonstrate that ULT is significantly more challenging. For example, test cases generated by LLMs only achieve 41.32\%, 45.10\%, 30.22\%, and 40.21\% for accuracy, statement coverage, branch coverage, and mutation score on average for all LLMs, respectively. These results are substantially lower than the corresponding metrics on TestEval (91.79\%, 92.18\%, 82.04\%, and 49.69\%) and PLT (47.07\%, 55.13\%, 40.07\%, and 50.80\%).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ULT (UnLeakedTestbench), a new benchmark for evaluating Large Language Models (LLMs) in generating unit tests from real-world Python functions. It addresses limitations in existing benchmarks like data contamination and structural simplicity of function code. ULT is built through a multi-stage curation process, ensuring high cyclomatic complexity and mitigating test case contamination.  The authors also create PLT (PreLeakedTestbench), a counterpart benchmark with deliberately leaked tests to study memorization. The paper conducts a large-scale empirical study with 12 LLMs, comparing their performance on ULT against existing benchmarks. The results demonstrate that ULT is more challenging, and shows a stronger correlation between code generation ability and test generation performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty stems primarily from the meticulous construction of the ULT benchmark. While using LLMs for test generation is not novel *per se*, the rigorous data decontamination process and focus on cyclomatic complexity represents a significant improvement. The creation and use of the PLT benchmark as a controlled environment to assess memorization vs. reasoning is also a strong point.
*   **Significance:** The significance lies in providing a more reliable and challenging evaluation framework for LLMs in unit test generation. Existing benchmarks often lead to inflated performance metrics, hindering accurate assessment. ULT has the potential to influence future research by setting a higher standard for evaluating LLMs in this domain. The demonstration that contaminated data skews results is important.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The benchmark construction process is well-defined and clearly explained. The rationale for each filtering stage (cyclomatic complexity, self-containment, testability, decontamination) is sound.
    *   **Controlled Experimentation:**  The PLT benchmark facilitates a controlled analysis of memorization, which is critical for understanding LLM capabilities.
    *   **Extensive Evaluation:** The large-scale empirical study involving 12 LLMs provides comprehensive evidence to support the claims.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and logically structured, making it easy to follow the methodology and understand the results.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Python-Specific:** The benchmark is limited to Python functions, potentially limiting the generalizability of the findings to other programming languages.
    *   **Dependency on Cyclomatic Complexity:** While cyclomatic complexity is a useful metric, relying solely on it to measure complexity may be oversimplified. Other complexity metrics (e.g., cognitive complexity, nesting depth) might offer additional insights. The authors did address this in the paper.
    *   **Implicit reliance on BigCodeBench: the use of BigCodeBench performance as the ground truth for coding ability feels like a strong assumption. A specific code generation benchmark built from the same corpus as ULT might have been preferable.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by addressing critical shortcomings in existing LLM test generation benchmarks. The construction of ULT, along with PLT for memorization analysis, and the extensive empirical evaluation provide valuable insights. While there are limitations, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses, and the paper has the potential to significantly influence future research and development in LLM-based unit test generation.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### Co-Reward: Self-supervised Reinforcement Learning for Large Language Model Reasoning via Contrastive Agreement
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.00410v1)
- **Authors**: Zizhuo Zhang, Jianing Zhu, Xinmu Ge, Zihua Zhao, Zhanke Zhou, Xuan Li, Xiao Feng, Jiangchao Yao, Bo Han
- **Abstract**: Although reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) shows promise in improving the reasoning ability of large language models (LLMs), the scaling up dilemma remains due to the reliance on human annotated labels especially for complex tasks. Recent alternatives that explore various self-reward signals exhibit the eliciting potential of LLM reasoning, but suffer from the non-negligible collapse issue. Inspired by the success of self-supervised learning, we propose \textit{Co-Reward}, a novel RL framework that leverages contrastive agreement across semantically analogical questions as a reward basis. Specifically, we construct a similar question for each training sample (without labels) and synthesize their individual surrogate labels through a simple rollout voting, and then the reward is constructed by cross-referring the labels of each question pair to enforce the internal reasoning consistency across analogical inputs. Intuitively, such a self-supervised reward-shaping mechanism increases the difficulty of learning collapse into a trivial solution, and promotes stable reasoning elicitation and improvement through expanding the input sample variants. Empirically, Co-Reward achieves superior performance compared to other self-reward baselines on multiple reasoning benchmarks and LLM series, and reaches or even surpasses ground-truth (GT) labeled reward, with improvements of up to $+6.8\%$ on MATH500 over GT reward on Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/tmlr-group/Co-Reward.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Co-Reward, a novel self-supervised reinforcement learning framework for improving the reasoning abilities of large language models (LLMs). Co-Reward addresses the scaling challenges associated with reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), which typically relies on human-annotated labels.  It leverages contrastive agreement across semantically analogical questions as a reward basis.  The framework constructs a similar question for each training sample, generates surrogate labels via rollout voting, and then uses cross-referencing of labels between question pairs to enforce internal consistency.  This approach aims to increase the difficulty of learning collapse into trivial solutions and promote stable reasoning through variant inputs. Experiments demonstrate that Co-Reward outperforms self-reward baselines across multiple reasoning benchmarks and LLM series, even reaching or surpassing ground-truth labeled reward performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a significant problem: the reliance on labeled data in RL fine-tuning of LLMs for reasoning. The core idea of using contrastive agreement, specifically across semantic variants of questions, is interesting and addresses a key weakness of existing self-rewarding methods which can easily collapse.  The approach has the potential to alleviate the need for extensive human annotation, which is a major bottleneck.

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using contrastive agreement on similar prompts is not entirely novel in the broader context of self-supervised learning, but its specific application to the RL fine-tuning of LLMs for reasoning tasks is a good contribution. The dual-path reward shaping based on cross-referencing the pseudo-labels also adds a unique aspect.

*   **Significance:** The experimental results are compelling and demonstrate the effectiveness of the Co-Reward framework.  The fact that Co-Reward can achieve performance competitive with or even surpassing ground-truth labeled reward methods on tasks like MATH500 indicates significant potential. The analyses of training stability, especially in mitigating the collapse behavior seen in other self-reward methods, further strengthens the paper's contribution. Moreover, the fact that the method can be used for test-time training independent of GT labels strengthens the practical applications of the study.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined problem and clear motivation.
    *   Technically sound approach with a strong intuitive basis.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across diverse reasoning benchmarks and LLM series.
    *   Detailed ablation studies and analyses, including discussion on reward hacking and stability.
    *   Case studies illustrating the qualitative benefits of Co-Reward.
    *   Publicly available code.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The additional computational overhead associated with generating multiple rephrased questions and performing rollout voting is a limitation, even though the paper claims the effectiveness. The method's efficiency could be improved.
    *   While the paper discusses reward hacking, further work is needed to mitigate it fully, especially for more complex and out-of-distribution question prompts.
    *   The choice of the majority voting mechanism may not be optimal in every context. While it is stated to be easy and effective, a better solution (which is discussed as a future work) may improve the performance and prevent potential limitations associated with majority-rule consensus.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    The Co-Reward framework could influence research in self-supervised RL for LLMs by providing a principled approach for leveraging contrastive agreement to improve reasoning. It has potential to spur further investigation into input-side based rewards and self-supervision techniques for improving reasoning of LLMs, including investigating different methods of generating variations in questions and different means of aggregating across runs.
Score: 7.5
Rationale: The paper presents a technically sound approach with solid empirical results. While the concept builds upon existing ideas in self-supervised learning and RL, it successfully adapts them to a specific and important problem area (reasoning in LLMs) and offers a promising solution. The method is computationally expensive and has some vulnerability, but the method does provide a better reasoning pathway than other common methods. The results are strong, and the analyses are valuable. The weaknesses are not critical enough to significantly discount the paper's contributions, but prevent a higher score.
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Sortblock: Similarity-Aware Feature Reuse for Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.00412v1)
- **Authors**: Hanqi Chen, Xu Zhang, Xiaoliu Guan, Lielin Jiang, Guanzhong Wang, Zeyu Chen, Yi Liu
- **Abstract**: Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) have demonstrated remarkable generative capabilities, particularly benefiting from Transformer architectures that enhance visual and artistic fidelity. However, their inherently sequential denoising process results in high inference latency, limiting their deployment in real-time scenarios. Existing training-free acceleration approaches typically reuse intermediate features at fixed timesteps or layers, overlooking the evolving semantic focus across denoising stages and Transformer blocks.To address this, we propose Sortblock, a training-free inference acceleration framework that dynamically caches block-wise features based on their similarity across adjacent timesteps. By ranking the evolution of residuals, Sortblock adaptively determines a recomputation ratio, selectively skipping redundant computations while preserving generation quality. Furthermore, we incorporate a lightweight linear prediction mechanism to reduce accumulated errors in skipped blocks.Extensive experiments across various tasks and DiT architectures demonstrate that Sortblock achieves over 2$\times$ inference speedup with minimal degradation in output quality, offering an effective and generalizable solution for accelerating diffusion-based generative models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Sortblock: Similarity-Aware Feature Reuse for Diffusion Model" introduces a novel training-free acceleration method for Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) that addresses the issue of slow inference speed in real-time applications.  The core idea is to dynamically cache block-wise features based on their similarity across adjacent timesteps, using a similarity metric to decide which blocks need recomputation and which can be reused.  The method incorporates a linear prediction mechanism to minimize error accumulation from reuse.  Experiments show that Sortblock achieves over 2x speedup with minimal degradation in output quality and outperforms other training-free baselines on image and video generation tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a practical idea of similarity-aware caching within DiTs. While feature reuse isn't entirely new in diffusion models or other generative models, the targeted application within DiTs, the dynamic adaptation of the recomputation ratio based on the evolving semantic focus during denoising, and the block-wise granularity are relatively novel. The linear prediction to mitigate accumulated error further enhances the approach. Prior work often used static strategies or coarser granularity, making this method more adaptive.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the practical acceleration of DiTs, making them more viable for real-time applications.  The training-free nature of the method is a significant advantage, as it avoids the computational costs and data requirements of distillation-based or retraining-based acceleration techniques.  The performance gains reported (2x speedup with minimal quality loss) are substantial and demonstrate the potential for wider adoption of DiTs in resource-constrained environments or latency-sensitive applications. The method seems generalizable across different DiT architectures and tasks, adding to its impact. However, it is important to acknowledge that acceleration techniques for diffusion models are a rapidly evolving field, and future methods may offer even greater speedups.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Training-free approach, readily applicable to existing DiT models.
    *   Adaptive caching strategy that dynamically adjusts recomputation ratio based on feature similarity.
    *   Incorporates a linear prediction mechanism to reduce error accumulation.
    *   Significant speedup with minimal quality degradation.
    *   Generalizable across different DiT architectures and tasks (image and video generation).
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation, including comparison with relevant baselines.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on cosine similarity as the sole metric for determining feature similarity could be a limitation.  Exploring other similarity metrics or combinations thereof might improve the accuracy of the caching strategy.
    *   The complexity of selecting hyperparameters, specifically for the polynomial fitting to adjust the recomputation ratio, could be a hurdle for practical implementation. A more robust and automated hyperparameter selection strategy would be beneficial.
    *   The paper focuses primarily on performance gains, with less emphasis on the theoretical underpinnings or a detailed analysis of why this similarity-aware strategy is particularly effective for DiTs. A more in-depth theoretical justification would strengthen the work.
    *   While the results are promising, a broader comparison with more cutting-edge diffusion acceleration methods, including both training-free and training-based approaches, would further contextualize the significance of Sortblock.
    *   Some analysis is limited. Fig 2, visualizing flux-dev blocks requires a more comprehensive explanation for full appreciation of the results.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the development of more efficient diffusion models and encourage the adoption of adaptive caching strategies.  The ideas presented could inspire further research on feature reuse techniques and dynamic resource allocation in generative models.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

I assign a score of 7.  Here's why:

*   **Noteworthy Contribution:** The paper offers a practical and effective solution to a critical problem in DiTs - slow inference speed.  The combination of similarity-aware caching and linear prediction demonstrates ingenuity and leads to impressive performance gains.

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While not a completely groundbreaking paradigm shift, the incremental improvements over existing methods justify the score. The focus on DiTs, block-wise granularity, and adaptive reuse strategy demonstrate a good understanding of the problem and a targeted solution.

*   **Solid Experimental Validation:** The experimental section is well-designed and includes comparisons with strong baselines. The reported results are convincing.

*   **Room for Improvement:** The score is not higher due to the previously mentioned weaknesses regarding a more thorough theoretical justification, further explorations of hyperparameter optimization, limited comparison with more recent state-of-the-art methods, and potentially simplistic similarity metrics.

The paper provides a tangible advancement in the field of diffusion model acceleration, but there is still space for more in-depth analysis and robustness improvements.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### DC-AE 1.5: Accelerating Diffusion Model Convergence with Structured Latent Space
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.00413v1)
- **Authors**: Junyu Chen, Dongyun Zou, Wenkun He, Junsong Chen, Enze Xie, Song Han, Han Cai
- **Abstract**: We present DC-AE 1.5, a new family of deep compression autoencoders for high-resolution diffusion models. Increasing the autoencoder's latent channel number is a highly effective approach for improving its reconstruction quality. However, it results in slow convergence for diffusion models, leading to poorer generation quality despite better reconstruction quality. This issue limits the quality upper bound of latent diffusion models and hinders the employment of autoencoders with higher spatial compression ratios. We introduce two key innovations to address this challenge: i) Structured Latent Space, a training-based approach to impose a desired channel-wise structure on the latent space with front latent channels capturing object structures and latter latent channels capturing image details; ii) Augmented Diffusion Training, an augmented diffusion training strategy with additional diffusion training objectives on object latent channels to accelerate convergence. With these techniques, DC-AE 1.5 delivers faster convergence and better diffusion scaling results than DC-AE. On ImageNet 512x512, DC-AE-1.5-f64c128 delivers better image generation quality than DC-AE-f32c32 while being 4x faster. Code: https://github.com/dc-ai-projects/DC-Gen.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DC-AE 1.5, a modified deep compression autoencoder family designed to accelerate the convergence of latent diffusion models (LDMs). The core problem addressed is that increasing the latent channel number in autoencoders (to improve reconstruction quality, especially at high spatial compression ratios) paradoxically slows down the convergence of the diffusion model and degrades generation quality. DC-AE 1.5 tackles this with two main innovations: (1) "Structured Latent Space," a training approach to impose a specific channel-wise structure where early channels capture object structure and later channels capture image details, and (2) "Augmented Diffusion Training," which adds diffusion training objectives to the object latent channels to speed up learning.  Experiments on ImageNet demonstrate faster convergence and better image generation quality compared to DC-AE.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**
    *   The idea of structuring the latent space in a channel-wise manner to separate object structure from details is a genuinely novel contribution. This contrasts with existing approaches that treat all latent channels equally.
    *   Augmented Diffusion Training, while perhaps less radically new, provides a targeted way to leverage the structured latent space.
*   **Significance/Impact:**
    *   The problem addressed is highly relevant. Faster convergence of diffusion models and the ability to use high compression ratios are critical for reducing training costs and enabling higher-resolution image generation. This will have a substantial effect on the field. The ability to boost quality at high compression is also very impactful.
    *   The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the effectiveness of DC-AE 1.5, with clear improvements in convergence speed and image quality. The scaling experiments further highlight the value in pushing the upper bound of latent diffusion models. It is a fairly robust set of experiments.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly identifies a problem and proposes a well-motivated solution.
    *   The "Structured Latent Space" concept is creative and practically effective.
    *   The experimental evaluation is thorough and supports the claims.
    *   The code is released, which makes the work reproducible.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The Augmented Diffusion Training strategy, while effective, could be argued as an incremental improvement building directly upon other diffusion training strategies. It lacks a degree of independent conceptual leap beyond the Structured Latent Space component.
    *   While ImageNet results are compelling, demonstrating generalizability to other datasets and tasks would strengthen the paper further.
    *   The analysis section of the paper could be deepened with more insight into why the proposed methods address sparsity and convergence speed effectively.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   This work can influence the design of future autoencoders for LDMs.
    *   The concept of structuring latent spaces could find applications in other generative modeling contexts beyond diffusion models.
    *   It can encourage exploration of targeted training objectives for specific parts of the latent space.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the clear novelty of structuring the latent space, the practical improvements in convergence and quality, and the thorough experimental evaluation, this paper represents a significant contribution to the field of latent diffusion models. The paper tackles a critical limitation in the area and provides a compelling solution. While the Augmented Diffusion Training is incremental, the impact is overall high. It seems the techniques can further advance the capabilities of LDMs.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Loop Invariant Generation: A Hybrid Framework of Reasoning optimised LLMs and SMT Solvers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.00419v1)
- **Authors**: Varun Bharti, Shashwat Jha, Dhruv Kumar, Pankaj Jalote
- **Abstract**: Loop invariants are essential for proving the correctness of programs with loops. Developing loop invariants is challenging, and fully automatic synthesis cannot be guaranteed for arbitrary programs. Some approaches have been proposed to synthesize loop invariants using symbolic techniques and more recently using neural approaches. These approaches are able to correctly synthesize loop invariants only for subsets of standard benchmarks. In this work, we investigate whether modern, reasoning-optimized large language models can do better. We integrate OpenAI's O1, O1-mini, and O3-mini into a tightly coupled generate-and-check pipeline with the Z3 SMT solver, using solver counterexamples to iteratively guide invariant refinement. We use Code2Inv benchmark, which provides C programs along with their formal preconditions and postconditions. On this benchmark of 133 tasks, our framework achieves 100% coverage (133 out of 133), outperforming the previous best of 107 out of 133, while requiring only 1-2 model proposals per instance and 14-55 seconds of wall-clock time. These results demonstrate that LLMs possess latent logical reasoning capabilities which can help automate loop invariant synthesis. While our experiments target C-specific programs, this approach should be generalizable to other imperative languages.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a hybrid framework for loop invariant generation, combining the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) with the formal verification power of SMT solvers.  The framework operates in a generate-and-check loop.  The LLM proposes a candidate invariant based on the program code and specifications. The SMT solver then verifies the proposed invariant.  If verification fails, the SMT solver provides a counterexample, which is fed back to the LLM to guide refinement of the invariant.  The authors integrate OpenAI's O1, O1-mini, and O3-mini models with the Z3 SMT solver and evaluate their approach on the Code2Inv benchmark, achieving 100% coverage and outperforming previous best results while requiring fewer LLM proposals and less wall-clock time.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in the *effective integration* of reasoning-optimized LLMs (specifically the OpenAI models) with an SMT solver in a tight generate-and-check loop for loop invariant generation. While the idea of using LLMs for invariant generation has been explored (e.g., in LEMUR), this paper demonstrates that the architecture is improved upon, and that newer, reasoning-optimized LLMs can dramatically improve performance. This includes better performance metrics on the Code2Inv dataset, while requiring less computational time and iterations.

*   **Significance:**  The paper's results are significant for several reasons:

    *   **Improved Coverage:** Achieving 100% coverage on the Code2Inv benchmark is a substantial improvement over previous approaches. This demonstrates the potential of LLMs to overcome limitations of traditional symbolic methods and even earlier neural approaches.
    *   **Efficiency:** The low iteration counts and wall-clock times suggest that reasoning-optimized LLMs can generate correct invariants with minimal guidance, indicating a potentially practical approach to automated verification.
    *   **Language Agnostic Potential:**  The use of an SMT-LIB compliant solver like Z3 and a language-agnostic pipeline facilitates the potential of extending to other imperative languages and more complicated datatypes.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The 100% coverage on Code2Inv and the significant reduction in iteration counts compared to previous LLM-based methods are compelling.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The paper clearly explains the generate-and-check loop and how LLM outputs are integrated with the SMT solver.
    *   **Solid Evaluation:** The paper provides detailed information on the experimental setup, the models used, and the metrics collected.
    *   **Well-contextualized Contribution**: The paper situates its contribution well with respect to previous LLM-based and traditional symbolic methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Benchmark limitations**: The Code2Inv benchmark, while standard, may not fully represent the complexity of real-world software. Generalization to more complex, nested loops and programs requires more validation.
    *   **Reliance on OpenAI Models:**  The results are heavily dependent on the specific reasoning capabilities of OpenAI's O1, O1-mini and O3-mini models.  The framework's robustness to changes in LLM architecture and performance is not clear.
    *   **SMT Solver Dependency:** Despite claiming language agnosticism, the process necessitates SMT encodings, inherently limiting the type of programs for which invariants can be effectively generated.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research in automated program verification by highlighting the promise of combining LLMs with formal methods. It suggests that LLMs are not just capable of syntactic manipulation but can also perform logical reasoning to some extent, which can be valuable for automating complex verification tasks.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper is a solid contribution to the field, demonstrating a significant advancement in loop invariant generation. The impressive empirical results, particularly the 100% coverage on the Code2Inv benchmark, are compelling. However, the reliance on specific OpenAI models and the limitations of the Code2Inv benchmark restrict its immediate impact. The reliance on a human curated SMT encoding (or the front-end tool) is also a limitation, hindering automated generalization. While promising, further research is needed to assess the framework's scalability and robustness to different LLMs and more complex programs. A higher score is warranted only after addressing these points, but the work has clearly made significant progress, particularly with regards to earlier LLM based invariant generation techniques.

- **Classification**: cs.LO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Automated Type Annotation in Python Using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.00422v1)
- **Authors**: Varun Bharti, Shashwat Jha, Dhruv Kumar, Pankaj Jalote
- **Abstract**: Type annotations in Python enhance maintainability and error detection. However, generating these annotations manually is error prone and requires extra effort. Traditional automation approaches like static analysis, machine learning, and deep learning struggle with limited type vocabularies, behavioral over approximation, and reliance on large labeled datasets. In this work, we explore the use of LLMs for generating type annotations in Python. We develop a generate check repair pipeline: the LLM proposes annotations guided by a Concrete Syntax Tree representation, a static type checker (Mypy) verifies them, and any errors are fed back for iterative refinement. We evaluate four LLM variants: GPT 4oMini, GPT 4.1mini (general-purpose), and O3Mini, O4Mini (reasoning optimized), on 6000 code snippets from the ManyTypes4Py benchmark. We first measure the proportion of code snippets annotated by LLMs for which MyPy reported no errors (i.e., consistent results): GPT 4oMini achieved consistency on 65.9% of cases (34.1% inconsistent), while GPT 4.1mini, O3Mini, and O4Mini each reached approximately 88.6% consistency (around 11.4% failures). To measure annotation quality, we then compute exact-match and base-type match accuracies over all 6000 snippets: GPT 4.1mini and O3Mini perform the best, achieving up to 70.5% exact match and 79.1% base type accuracy, requiring under one repair iteration on average. Our results demonstrate that general-purpose and reasoning optimized LLMs, without any task specific fine tuning or additional training can be effective in generating consistent type annotations.They perform competitively with traditional deep learning techniques which require large labeled dataset for training. While our work focuses on Python, the pipeline can be extended to other optionally typed imperative languages like Ruby
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for automated type annotation in Python. The authors present a "generate-check-repair" pipeline where an LLM proposes type annotations, a static type checker (Mypy) verifies them, and error messages are fed back to the LLM for iterative refinement. They evaluate several LLM variants (GPT-40-Mini, GPT-4.1-mini, O3-Mini, and O4-Mini) on the ManyTypes4Py benchmark. The results demonstrate that even without task-specific fine-tuning, general-purpose and reasoning-optimized LLMs can generate consistent type annotations that are competitive with existing deep learning approaches. The approach is also potentially applicable to other gradually typed languages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**
